ton in "File->Python" or clicking the help icon for the method in the method selection or the method
itself, as indicated by the green box in the screenshots below:
16.1 2D Least squares matching (LSM)
2D Least Squares matching (LSM) is a method for determining 2D velocity fields in highly seeded
flows in water and air. The input data consists of double-frame images or of time-resolved single-
PAGE|472
frame images and the output data are equally spaced vector fields. Interrogation areas from within
the image are analyzed to determine local affine transformations.
16.1.1 Background information
The fundamental theorem of Helmholtz states that every infinitesimal motion of a fluid element can
be decomposed in translation, rotation and deformation. In the last decades several investigations
have been performed to experimentally describe these fluid motions. In classical PIV based on cor-
relation techniques, 2D cross-correlation is most frequently applied to extract the zero order trans-
lational velocity components neglecting the higher order terms of rotation and deformation. The
assumption is that the flow field is smooth and not significantly influenced by rotational or shear dis-
placements, thus yielding the zero-order translational displacement field with an additional meas-
urement uncertainty due to neglecting the higher-order terms. The measurement uncertainty can
be reduced by window deformation techniques, which require manipulation of the raw particle
images. The higher-order motion terms are then indirectly estimated on discrete grids by finite dif-
ference schemes. The assumption is that the higher order fluid motion of an element is only
affected by the translational velocity components of the neighboring elements.
In contrast to correlation based techniques, LSM shifts, rotates and stretches a fluid element. For
this purpose, the LSM algorithm iteratively compares gray value information of an interrogation
area in the first time step with the gray value information in the second time step. This is an iterative
least squares procedure applying a proper transformation on the interrogation areas. In 2D this res-
ults in six transformation parameters. The advantage of LSM is that whilst calculating the zero
order translational velocities, the first order terms of motion are simultaneously optimized increas-
ing the accuracy of the velocity field. The resulting displacement gradient tensor includes para-
meters like rotation, shear and strain of the interrogation area resulting from the particle
displacement within the area.
2D LSM performs a geometric and radiometric transformation between two successive states of
the same system. In the case of a gray value filled interrogation area its state is a gray value dis-
tribution of the pixel elements. For this purpose, the transformation is optimized such that the gray
value differences between a template area and a search area reach a minimum. Compared with
conventional 2D cross-correlation, LSM considers the deformation of a fluid element for the cal-
culation of the displacements. This results in a more accurate calculation of the velocity field. The
velocity gradient tensor and as result the rotation and deformation rate tensor can be calculated
without applying central difference schemes. In LSM all this is done without manipulation of the raw
particle images.
16.1.2 Usage
The use of 2D LSM is very similar to that of standard cross-correlation. The recipe dialog is shown
below. You can define a Threshold level such that only areas of the image are used which show a
variation in intensity above the chosen treshold. The Interrogation are size can be chosen inde-
pendently in x- and y-direction, and any odd number may be chosen. Also the spacing
(Shift) between the vectors (i.e. overlapping of the interrogation areas) can be chosen inde-
pendently in both directions, and any even number of pixels is allowed.. The start position (Grid
Start) can be chosen to be different from the lower left corner. The size of the vector field can be
specified with the parameter Grid Width Height. These two last parameters allow to compute the
vector field on the smaller area then the whole image, if desired.
The Search factor defines the size of the second interrogation area, which the first interrogation
area is matched to. The iterative procedure is stopped when all parameters have converged or
when the number of Maximum iterations has been reached. In the latter case these vectors may
be rejected by checking the corresponding check box. A significance test can be applied to check
if the calculated shear, rotation and scale parameters are significant. Please note, that in case they
are not significant they will be replaced with zero-entries in the data set.
As an iterative method LSM needs starting values for the affine parameters that are to be determ-
ined. If no foreknowledge exists about the velocity field, all parameters are initialized with Zero. To
speed-up the processing and reach better convergence behavior the LSM process can be ini-
tialized with the results of a preceding cross-correlation step (Use cross-correlation as input). To
PAGE|473
avoid false initialization by outliers, a Universal Outlier Detection validation can be enabled. Its
parameters (Neighborhood size and Normalized Residual) can be adjusted. An alternative
method to provide initialization is the Pyramid approach. It is usually faster to use the cross-cor-
relation as an input, and it is recommend to use this initialization to ensure the reliability of the cal-
culated vector field.

16.2 2-Frame 2D PTV
The two-frame, two-dimensional Particle Tracking Velocimetry (2-Frame 2D PTV) algorithm can
achieve high accuracy velocity measurements in double-frame images with moderate to high
particle densities, typical to raw PIV images. The 2-Frame 2D PTV analysis is currently only pos-
sible after an “Adaptive PIV” analysis in the following situations:
• A single Adaptive PIV vector map
• An ensemble of Adaptive PIV vector maps
16.2.1 1. How it works
The Adaptive PIV analysis iteratively deforms the interrogation windows between consecutive
frames in order to get a better correlation peak during cross-correlation analysis. In addition to this,
from DynamicStudio version 5.0 (2016a) onwards, the PIV Uncertainty analysis can perform
particle matching for each interrogation window after the final deformation step. The particle match-
ing algorithm implemented here is a modified version of Sciacchitano et al. (2013)1 , used for PIV
uncertainty quantification. In the current implementation the particle disparity vectors calculated for
individual particle pairs are stored after each Adaptive PIV analysis. This information is later used
for uncertainty quantification and for 2-Frame 2D PTV analysis methods.
16.2.2 2. Graphical User Interface
The 2-Frame 2D PTV GUI contains user inputs and metrics on the left and the visual information on
the right. The user inputs are provided by sliders and by using the keyboard. One can:
• Browse different images in the ensemble
• Set a threshold value for background noise removal
• Perform a second pass for particle matching (optional)
• Perform outlier detection (optional)
• Review different metrics
The image matrix on the right contains the following information:
• The input image
• The input vector map calculated by Adaptive PIV processing
• The matched pairs in the 1st pass
• The matched pairs in the 2nd pass (if selected)
1PIV uncertainty quantification by image matching, by Andrea Sciacchitano, Bernhard Wieneke and Fulvio
Scarano http://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/0957-0233/24/4/045302/meta
PAGE|475
16.2.3 3. Typical use
Image Index: In some situations it is not desirable to select the 1st image pair to test different ana-
lysis settings. The slider can be used to select any image pair (or vector map) in the ensemble.
Threshold value: A threshold value can be set to ignore background noise in the image pair before
2nd pass particle detection. If set to 0, raw images are used for particle detection, otherwise particle
images with a greyvalue below the threshold will be ignored.
2nd Pass Particle matching (Optional): In certain situations (e.g. in highly turbulent flows) it is
desirable to perform a second pass to increase the number of matches between two consecutive
frames. If selected, the algorithm finds each unmatched particle in frame 1, takes the average dis-
placement of the neighboring vectors in a certain radius (consensus sample radius set by the user)
and searches for a match in frame 2 within a search radius (once again set by the user). The con-
sensus sample radius and the search radius is defined a percentage of the final interrogation area
size and the consensus vector size, respectively.
Outliers: Similar to PIV analysis, outliers may occur in the PTV analysis. The detection and replace-
ment of outliers are performed similar to Universal Outlier Detection for PIV data, using the
guidelines provided by Duncan et al. (2010)1 . The detection threshold and normalization levels
here are actually weighted inversely by the distance of the neighboring vectors in the unstructured
grid.
1Universal outlier detection for particle image velocimetry (PIV) and particle tracking velocimetry (PTV)
data, by J Duncan, D Dabiri, J Hove and M Gharib, http://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/0957-
0233/21/5/057002/meta
PAGE|476
When a change is registered in one of the settings a red circle appears on the images in order to
indicate that the algorithm is working on the calculation with the new settings.
Metrics: In order to give an idea about the success of the algorithm, some metrics are provided:
• Number of particles detected in frame 1 and 2
• Number of matches found in the 1st pass
• Additional number of matches found in the 2nd pass
• Total number of matches found after both passes
• Match ratios after the 1st and 2nd passes
Typical results: It is expected that the PTV results are more accurate and have more resolution
than the PIV results since they operate on individual particles instead of a collection of particles.
PIV (left) and PTV (right) results of a toroidal vortex mid-plane calculated using synthetic particles
is shown below:
16.3 Time-Resolved 2D PTV
The Time-Resolved 2D PTV analysis method is a 2D velocimetry technique and reconstructs the
particles trajectories of the measured scene.
It can be performed on particles inside a light sheet illuminated volume that is captured using single
frame camera recordings. The analysis techniques is designed to be fast and for low to moderate
seeding densities. The quality of the results are greatly dependent on the thickness of the light
sheet, A thinner light sheet reduces tracking ambiguities that arise from the fact that the method is a
2D method
PAGE|477
The Time-Resolved 2D PTV consists of two main parts, in a first step the particles are detected and
in the second the particle tracking algorithms matches the particles in time. For more information on
the algorithm please see Analysis Process section.
16.3.1 Applying the Time-Resolved 2D PTV
For all Velocimetry methods it is important that the image quality is of a good level, and that the
images are preferably pre-processed and cleaned to a degree where only the particles in the
images have a non-zero gray-value.
.
16.3.2 Interface and settings
The user interface for Time-Resolved 2D PTV is split into 2 sections:
The first part of the recipe contains the settings for the tracking:
The "Tracking Area". Here the dimensions and the center of the tracking area with respect to the
image is entered in pixels.
The "Search Parameters". Here limitations to the initial particle association can be made to inform
the tracking algorithm of a direction of a mean flow for example
The "Image Processing". Where simple image processing can be applied to the raw images to
enhance the tracking results.
The Second part of the interface contain the display which is a preview of the current processed
image, based on the chosen processing level in the Image Processing section described above,
and a bounding box it showing the area to be processed, based on the selection in the Tracking
area section described above.
PAGE|478
The tracking method used is the same as the one used for 3D tracking. For further information on
the tracking method please refer to the Analysis process section of the 3D PTV tracking analysis
documentation.
3D Particle Tracking Velocimetry.htm
16.4 3D Particle Tracking Velocimetry
The 3D Particle Tracking Velocimetry (3D PTV) analysis method is a volumetric technique and
reconstructs the particles trajectories of the measured scene.
It can be performed of particles inside a 3D illuminated volume that is captured using single frame
camera recordings from at least 3 different camera viewing angles. However, the more cameras
are used, the higher the seeding concentration can be, since, ambiguities in the particle tri-
angulation can be reduced. The analysis techniques is designed to be fast and for low to moderate
seeding densities. The seeding density is typically measured in particles per pixel (ppp), which is
measured on the camera sensor plane. Typically for a four-camera set-up the maximum seeding
density with 3D Particle Tracking is about 0.01ppp. that means independent form the measurement
depth the number of particles allowed in the volume is constant. This results in a better spatial res-
olution the thinner the volume is. If higher seeding densities need to be analyzed by a tracking
approach, the "Tomographic Particle Tracking Velocimetry" on page 913 can cope with about twice
the seeding density.
The 3D PTV consists of two main parts, in a first step the particles are triangulated to get a 3D loc-
ations, one the 3D locations of the particles are known, the particle tracking algorithms matches the
particles in time. For more information on the algorithm please see Analysis Process section.
PAGE|479
16.4.1 Applying the 3D Particle Tracking Velocimetry
For all Volumetric Velocimetry methods it is important that the image quality is of a good level, and
that the images are pre-processed and cleaned to a degree where only the particles in the images
have a non-zero gray-value.
The 3D PTV needs input form the acquired images and the spatial camera calibrations. All of them
need to be marked with a space-bar, as shown in the image below
.
Note: with the new release of DynamicStudio, the only calibration model available for this method is
a Pinhole calibration model. Support for a 3rd order Model will be introduced at a later stage. For
more information on the calibration model please and the Multi Camera Calibration please take a
look to this section: "Multi Camera Calibration" on page 765
16.4.2 Interface and settings
The user interface for 3D PTV is split into 4 section: The display which is a preview window with
one of the images and a bounding box around it showing the to be reconstructed volume. The
volume settings which are defined in the 2nd part the "Tracking Volume", here the dimensions and
the center of the volume with respect to the calibrated volume is entered. In the third section, the
tracking and triangulation parameters are defined. While in the fours and last section, basic image
processing can be applied to the raw images to enhance the tracking results.
PAGE|480
Tracking Volume
In the Tracking Volume section, the volume is defined in which the actual PTV is perfromed. Only
particles within this volume will be considered. The example above shows the volume has a size in
X,Y,Z of 80 x 46 x 20 mm. the center of this volume (40 mm from the left, 23 mm from the top and
10 mm form the back), is the point that references to the target location. In the given case the cen-
ter of the volume happens to be to the center of the calibrated area. Thus if the volume would not be
centered around 0|0|0 coordinate from the calibrate, here the offset is defined.
Tracking Parameters
Tracking parameters define the particle triangulation and particle tracking properties.
l Maxdeviationfromepipolarlineinpx:Defines the distance a particle is allowed to be away
from the epipolar line in the other camera.
l Projectedpointsearchradiusinpx:Defines the distance a particle candidate is allowed to be
away from the projected particle location in order to make a match.
l Maxmovement/framemm:Defines the search radius for the nearest neighbor criteria in mm
for the x,y,z direction this is how much a particle can move from one frame to the next.
l Min.tracklength:Defines the minimum duration of a particle track. Here one should set it to at
least 4 or 5 to avoid ghost or false tracks.
Image Processing Parameters
Tracking parameters define the particle triangulation and particle tracking properties.
l Displayimage:Defines which image time step is previewed.
l Imagethreshold:Defines a threshold value below which every gray value is clamped to 0.
l Imageblur:Defines if the raw images should be smoothed or not and what the kernel size for
the blur should be.
16.4.3 Analysis process
The analysis section is divided into two main sections.
PAGE|481
1. The particle triangulation, where the 3D positions of the particles are determined
2. The particle tracking, where the 3D positions are connected in time to get the trajectories
During the triangulation phase first all 2D particles centroids in the images are located. before these
2D locations are projected along their epipolar lines into the other cameras. On the next cameras
potential particle candidates are found, these are potential 3D locations which are close to the epi-
polar line of a given particle. This distance in the 3D PTV menu is referred to as the "Max. deviation
from epipolar line". This corresponds to the closest distance from a particle centroid to the epipolar
line.
Using additional cameras the 3D candidates are verified using projection into the remaining cam-
eras where the projected particle position needs to have a match within the specified search radius.
This entire procedure is shown in the sketch below:
For the tracking phase the 3D particle locations are combined to 3D trajectories. Here, it is import-
ant that always combine the same particle for the tracking process in time. A so-called Four-Frame
approach is used in order to optimize the tracking. For time-steps T1 until T2 from the given
example below, no known velocity information exists. Hence, the trajectory needs to be initialized
by the nearest neighbor criteria. Which means that the closest 3D locations of a particle within the
predefined search radius in x,y, and z-direction is the selected match for the next time-step. In the
menu this is referred to as "Max movement / frame". The following sketch shows the search area in
the red circle.
PAGE|482
.
After a first velocity is known, the search radius in the next time step can be narrowed down since
the velocity is known. Ones we have found a particle at T3 the acceleration can also be used, to nar-
row down the search radius again for the fourth time step T4.
With many particle candidates for the track present a criteria for ambiguities for established tracks
is needed. This avoids that particles which earlier were connected to another track, that might fit
better to another track can be reassigned to this one. Therefore a fitness function is used. In case
that multiple particles are within a search radius the fitness function always picks the one closest to
the projected centroid location. In case this particle already is part of another track this track needs
to be re-evaluated afterwards.
16.5 Active Image Stitching
As implied by the name Active Image Stitching attempts to stitch together images from multiple
cameras to one larger image, stitching together the Field of View from each of the cameras.
Normally the cameras are placed side by side with Fields of View that overlap slightly along adjoin-
ing edges.
Examples shown here stitch together images from two cameras, but you can use more.
16.5.1 Input to Image Stitching
Apart from the parent images you need spatial calibrations for each of the cameras used. Active
Image Stitching is designed for and works best with spatial calibrations made with the "Active Tar-
get" on page 440, but you can use other calibrations as well, provided they are made using Dynam-
icStudio 7.3 or later. It does not matter which Imaging model you use (DLT, Pinhole or Polynomial,
see "Multi Camera Calibration" on page 765 for details), since the Active Image Stitching directly
use the list of corresponding marker positions in object space and image plane. Older versions of
DynamicStudio did not store this information along with the camera calibrations and they can thus
not be used for Active Image Stitching.
Using the "Active Target" on page 440 you can let the acquisition system automatically perform the
calibration immediately after acquiring the images and store the resulting camera calibrations dir-
ectly to the DynamicStudio Database without storing the images. You can of course also store the
calibration images as usual and then initiate the calibration manually.
In the two examples below the left one use spatial camera calibrations performed by the acquisition
system, the right one is based on calibrations initiated by the user:
PAGE|483
In both cases you then right-click either of the parent camera images, select 'Analyze' and then pick
'Active Image Stitching' in the Image Processing Category:
16.5.2 Recipe for Active Image Stitching
The recipe for Active Image Stitching has a central preview and a few parameters at the top
PAGE|484
In this example we're stitching together two of the images from which the spatial camera cal-
ibrations were made. Both have been inverted and one made darker than the other so it is clearly
visible where each of the pixels in the stitched image comes from. In practice you will normally try to
align both Field of View and grayscale intensities from each of the cameras to minimize differences
across the stitching boundary, but for this example grayscale differences help to better explain the
handling of the overlap area.
The parameter 'pixels per mm' define how far apart pixels in the stitched image are. If you press the
'Default' button in the right hand side the system will compute a value that match the resolution of
the parent images reasonably well and preserve most of the information from the original images.
You can change the value if you want, but less than half of the suggested value will lead to severe
loss of data whereas doubling it will just generate larger images that take longer to process, but
does not contain any additional information.
The other recipe option 'Select Dominant Camera' controls the handling of grayscale values in the
overlap area. By default this option is unchecked and grayscale values in the overlap area are
simply the average of values from the parent images.
If you enable the option a drop down list will become active in which you must choose one of the
cameras as the dominant.
Example below is the same as above, but zoomed in on the overlap area and showing the output
with the left camera as dominant, the (default) average and the right camera chosen as dominant:
PAGE|485
16.5.3 Pitfalls
Like most other multi-camera systems Active Image Stitching rely on spatial camera calibrations
and assumes that measurement images are acquired exactly where the target was when the cal-
ibration was performed. This means that accurate stitching require careful alignment of the target to
e.g. the laser lightsheet if such is used for illumination. In case of misalignment some of the meas-
urements will in fact be performed closer to or further away from the cameras than assumed by the
stitching routine. Due to perspective this will lead to misalignment between the Fields of View in the
overlap area. Stitching PIV images the particles may become blurry or even appear 'double' if/when
using the default averaging of grayscale values in the overlap area. If you select a dominant cam-
era instead particle images will remain as sharp as they were in the parent images, but a particle tra-
jectory may 'jump' sideways when it moves from the Field of View of one camera to another.
16.6 Adaptive PIV
The Adaptive PIV method is an automatic and adaptive method for calculating velocity vectors
based on particle image pairs.
It is inspired by the 2010 thesis by Raf Theunissen 1: "Adaptive image interrogation for PIV - Applic-
ation to complex flows and interfaces".
The method can iteratively adjust the size, shape and location of the individual interrogation areas
(IA) in order to adapt to local seeding densities and flow velocities and gradients.
The method also includes options to apply window functions, frequency domain filtering as well as
validation using Universal Outlier Detection.
The figure below shows the recipe for the Adaptive PIV method.
1Raf Theunissen (2010):
"Adaptive resolution in PIV image analysis - Application to complex flows and interfaces"
Delft University, The Netherlands & VUB, Belgium, February 2010
ISBN: 978-2-87516-000-3
PAGE|486
The dialog has two main parts, the image view and the recipe settings seen in upper section of the
dialog. In the following the available settings will be described.
16.6.1 Interrogation areas
On the ‘Interrogation areas’ tab it is possible to adjust the layout of the IA sampling grid.
The number of interrogation areas (IAs) and the spacing between their center positions is determ-
ined by the parameter ‘Grid Step Size’. The grid step is specified as number of pixels from one IA to
its neighbor. Grid Step Size directly control the spacing between vectors so the smaller it is the
more vectors will be computed. If grid step is smaller than IA Size, the IA’s will overlap. Grid step
size is specified independently for both horizontal and vertical direction.
The Adaptive PIV method can automatically determine an appropriate IA size to use for each indi-
vidual IA, but specified minimum and/or maximum IA sizes limits the range. These are also visu-
alized by the green boxes in the center of the image view.
The first iteration will always use the largest IA size allowed, while subsequent iterations is allowed
to reduce IA sizes where particle density is high enough to justify it.
Minimum IA size is also used to determine the location of vectors; Both horizontally and vertically
there will be as many vectors as possible within the area covered (full image or ROI); Grid Step
Size determine the distance between neighbor vectors, while Minimum IA Size determine how
close to the borders a vector may be located. When centered around the vector position, the min-
imum sized IA is guaranteed to be completely inside the image area processed, while the
PAGE|487
maximum IA Size centered around the same location may in fact extend beyond the image (or ROI)
borders.
By default Adaptive PIV will analyze the entire input image, but with the check box 'Use only selec-
ted region' you can chose a region to analyze by adjusting a rectangle inside the image display.
With the check box 'Use only cross correlation' you can turn off all adaptivity and just perform clas-
sic textbook cross-correlation with fixed interrogation areas and no offset or iterations. It is useful
for a quick test or perhaps as benchmark against which other approaches may be compared, but it
is not recommended for quality results. If this check box is selected a number of the features
described below will be switched off.
With the check box 'Analyse selected region only' you can specify an region of interest in the image
which should be analyzed.
With the check box 'Enable GPU acceleration' you can select if you want to use the GPU for the PIV
computation or not. Please note that an NVIDIA GPU with Compute Capability 5.2 or higher and at
least 2 GB memory is required. The small information icon next to the checkbox indicates that a
supported GPU has been detected on the local machine. If no supported GPU has been detected,
the information icon will be replaced with a warning icon. You may hover the mouse over the icon
for details.
A second way to speed up the computation is to use distributed analysis for Adaptive PIV. This will
also work if you have only a local PC available. Using distributed analysis, even with only a local
PC, will allow DynamicStudio to compute multiple datasets in parallel which will usually result in
improved performance. When GPU acceleration is enabled, hosts with a supported GPU will com-
bine CPU and GPU computations for optimal performance, while hosts without a supported GPU
will continue to use CPU computations only. Please note that depending on the selected Adaptive
PIV settings, the contribution of the GPU to the computation can differ.
16.6.2 Windowing and Filtering
The tab ‘Window/Filter’ is used to apply a spatial windowing and/or frequency filtering function as
part of the FFT based cross-correlation.
The conventional Window and Filter Functions are the same as for other correlation methods ("Cor-
relation option Window/Filter" on page 997, but the checkmark 'Use Wall Windowing' is unique to
Adaptive PIV:
Wall Windowing can be used when a mask is available to identify the location of walls. The Mask
must be preselected before entering the Adaptive PIV Recipe ("Selection (Input to Analysis)" on
page 468).You can use either a regular 'Mask' ("Define Mask" on page 571) or an ordinary image
(typically derived from acquired images through Image Processing). If you wish to use an ordinary
image you need to enable it's Custom Property 'Use as Mask' in order for it to be recog-
nized/accepted as a mask ("Custom Properties" on page 468). Zero-valued pixels in the Mask
image will be treated as 'Wall', while nonzero pixels will be interpreted as 'Fluid'.
The purpose of Wall windowing is to mitigate wall bias; Correlation measures the average dis-
placement/velocity of particles within the interrogation area (IA). There are (normally) no particles
inside walls, so when an IA extends into a wall resulting displacements/velocities may be biased by
particles far from the wall, that generally move faster than particles close to the wall. Wall win-
dowing attempts to mitigate this effect by masking also the particles far from the wall, so remaining
PAGE|488
particles are symmetrically distributed around the center of the IA. Figures below show an Inter-
rogation Area (frame 1 only) and resulting Correlation map with and without wall windowing
applied:
Top left shows an Interrogation Area where the lower left extend into a wall (shown in blue). Top
right shows the result from correlating two such IA's.
Bottom left shows the same IA with Wall Windowing applied; The zero values in the lower left have
been rotated 180 degrees around the IA center to mask out the particles in the upper right as well;
Remaining pixels are now symmetrically distributed around the IA center and bottom right shows
the resulting correlation map.
With Wall Windowing applied there is a risk that no particles remain from which to compute a cor-
relation in which case there will be no velocity estimate in this location, but if a vector is found it will
be unbiased.
16.6.3 Validation
The validation is used to prevent outliers from disturbing the iterations and thus the velocity meas-
urements. The validation is done by first applying peak validation on the image cross-correlation
and secondly by comparing each vector to its neighbors using the Universal Outlier Detection
algorithm.
Three peak validation schemes are proposed in order to invalidate vectors based on the image cor-
relation peaks:
o Peak Height
if the Peak Height validation is enabled, then only the correlation peaks above the specified
value will be retained as valid.
PAGE|489
o Peak Height Ratio
if the Peak Height validation is enabled, then the ratio between the two highest correlation
peaks is calculated. This ratio must be higher than the specified value in order to validate the
calculated displacement. Typical value for Peak Height ratio is 1.2.
o S/N-Ratio
if S/N ratio is enabled, first the noise level in the correlation plane is evaluated by the Root
Mean Square of the negative correlation values. If the ratio between the correlation Peak
Height and the noise level is above the specified value, then the calculated displacement is con-
sidered valid.
We recommend using Peak Height or S/N-Ratio validation criterion. Indeed, if the Interrogation
Area only contains noise the ratio between the two highest peak may still be quite high.
If either Peak validation fails the corresponding vector will be Rejected. Later when the "Universal
Outlier Detection" on page 936 is performed, and substitution is enabled, the rejected vector may
be replaced with the median of valid neighbor vectors.
After the first and intermediate iterations validation and substitution is mandatory, but after the last
iteration the user may choose not to validate at all, to validate, but not substitute rejected vectors or
to both validate and substitute.
16.6.4 Adaptivity
The ‘Adaptivity’ tab contains settings that will affect the adaptive adjustment that is iteratively
applied to each IA.
Adapt IA Size to Particle Density
The initial correlation will always use the specified maximum IA size allowed to get a reasonable
first guess of displacements to be used for IA offsets later.
If adaptivity to particle density is switched on each of the following iterations will determine IA size
from a local estimate of particle density, aiming to get the desired number of particles within the IA.
If adaptivity to particle density is switched off each of the following iterations will divide the IA size
by two.
Regardless of settings the IA Size will never be smaller than the minimum or larger than the max-
imum specified on the 'Interrogation areas' Tab.
When switched on two parameters adjust how the particle density adaptivity works:
Particle detection limit:
Determines how a particle is detected. A gray scale peak must rise this many times above the noise
floor to be counted as a particle.
Desired # of particles/IA:
PAGE|490
Will affect the size of the interrogation areas by specifying how many particles an IA should nom-
inally contain.
Adapt IA Shape to Velocity Gradients
When IA Shape adaptivity is switched off Interrogation Areas will remain rectangular (commonly
square), so IA offset is used to account for translation, but gradients are not accounted for.
When IA Shape adaptivity is switched on Interrogation Areas will both be offset to account for trans-
lation and distorted to account for velocity gradients:
In the vicinity of a point where both velocities and velocity gradients are known, the local velocity
field can be approximated by a Taylor expansion:
-or in a single matrix expression:
-which is an affine transform where velocities (u0,v0,w0) and all gradients are evaluated in the ref-
erence point (x0,y0,z0) and (Δx,Δy,Δz)=(x-x0,y-y0,z-z0).
If we multiply with Δt, the time between the two particle images, velocities become displacements,
which can be measured in pixels and if (x,y,z) positions are also measured in pixels all the gradi-
ents become dimensionless.
In 2D this simplifies to (omitting higher order terms):
-where all (u,v) and their gradients now describe displacements in pixel and (Δx,Δy) describe dis-
tances in pixel from the center of the interrogation area, where displacements and gradients apply.
Since both (u,v) displacements and (x,y) positions are in pixel the spatial gradients become
pixel/pixel, i.e. dimensionless.
Visualizing each of the affine transform parameters they represent displacement patterns such as
these:
PAGE|491
-where green arrows represent displacement direction, blue polygons the 'upstream' IA on frame 1
and orange polygons the 'downstream' IA on frame 2. Dotted gray squares represent nominal IA
position and shape halfway between frame 1 & 2.
Figures above represent positive parameters in a regular Cartesian coordinate system with x/u pos-
itive to the right and y/v positive up. For negative parameter values arrows switch direction and
blue/orange polygons swap places.
Figures on the far right represent mean flow velocities that apply equally in the entire Interrogation
Area. The others represent gradients; -The top middle represent shear, where particles at the top of
the IA move faster/longer than particles at the bottom and the top left represent acceleration, where
particles in the right side of the IA move faster/longer than particles in the left side. Figures at the
bottom represent similar patterns for vertical velocity/displacement of particles within the Inter-
rogation Area.
Combining displacements and gradients with different signs in different ways the affine transform
can describe velocity/displacement distributions such as these:
The leftmost combine horizontal & vertical translations U & V followed by accelerations dU/dX &
dV/dY in the middle and finally shears dU/dY & dV/dX to the right. In the top row parameters have
identical signs, in the bottom row they have opposite signs. As before switching sign on all gradi-
ents will flip the direction of the green arrows and cause the blue and orange polygons to swap
places on frame 1 & 2 respectively.
Apart from IA translations various IA deformations are illustrated such as expansion, contraction,
rotation and saddle points.
The affine parameters can of course combine in a multitude of ways to describe the local flow field
in the vicinity of each vector.
PAGE|492
In practice an imaginary Interrogation Area is created at a point in time halfway between the two
image frames being correlated. Using the affine transform this IA is mapped backward half a time
step to look in frame 1 where each pixel is coming from. Likewise it is mapped forward by half a
time step to see where in frame 2 each pixel is going:
For simplicity the example above shows the forward/backward mapping of the 'Halfway' Inter-
rogation Area in the case of a purely horizontal flow with mean flow U0>0 and gradient dU/dY>0.
If the displacements and gradients used are correct, mapping Frame 1 & 2 forward and backward
by half a timestep should create two identical IAs, which of course produces a correlation peak at
(0,0). If not the location of the correlation peak is used as a correction to the previously estimated
displacement and the corrected displacement is used for the next iteration.
Gradients in the affine transform are estimated by comparing neighbors in the vector map and spa-
tial smoothing is applied to reduce the sensitivity to noise. Higher order gradients are even more
sensitive to noise, so a Taylor expansion of the local flow field including e.g. second order terms
makes little sense.
When IA Shape adaptivity is switched on the gradients are limited to below a certain (absolute)
value, that defaults to 0.1.
Imagine for example a shear of dU/dY=0.1 applied on a standard 32x32 Interrogation Area: This
would mean that particles at the top of the IA moves 32x0.1=3.2 pixels further than pixels at the bot-
tom. (In practice particles at the IA edge will be clipped, so 30x0.1=3.0 pixels is probably more real-
istic). This matches quite well with recommended particle image diameters of 2-3 pixels.
If gradients are much stronger than this odds are that in the very first correlation, where we have no
prior knowledge of velocities or gradients, particle images do not correlate at any displacement, so
we will not be able to get a meaningful vector map from which to start the iteration. For larger inter-
rogation areas it gets even worse and with smaller interrogation areas we may have too few
particles within the IA or find no matches with which to correlate if displacements are larger than
25% of the IA size.
Apart from a limit on the magnitude of each gradient there is also a limit on the combined mag-
nitude, which defaults to 0.2. Please note that this value corresponds to every single gradient being
at the limit 0.1, so in effect the default value has no effect here. In most cases gradients will be smal-
ler and you are welcome to reduce the limits further, but for reasons explained above it is not recom-
mended to expand them beyond the default values.
Iteration control
Convergence limit (pixel):
A stop criteria for the adaptive iteration. When the translational part of the IA shape correction is
less than the specified convergence limit, the iteration is stopped for the given IA. It may continue
for other interrogation areas.
Max # of iterations :
Specifies the maximum number of iteration to perform. The analysis will stop after the specified
number of iteration, no matter if the analysis of the IA has converged or not.
PAGE|493
16.6.5 Diagnostic
The top right part of the recipe contain a 'Diagnostic' tool, allowing the user to single step through
the iterative procedure, inspecting the interrogation areas, correlations and vectors after each iter-
ation in the adaptive process.
By pressing the ‘Step’ button, a single step in the iterative process will be calculated, and the result
will be shown.
Below we zoom in on a 5x5 neighborhood of interrogation areas and corresponding vectors:
The left part of the figure above show results after the first iteration and the right part show the
same area after the second iteration.
The blue rectangles illustrate the interrogation areas, possibly scaled down to prevent them from
overlapping in the display. Comparing the left and right image, the IA size was clearly reduced from
the first to the second iteration. When an iteration has converged the blue rectangle will turn green.
The actual size, shape and offset of the interrogation areas is shown when the mouse hovers over
a specific vector. In the left image above the red rectangle shows the actual IA size and in the right
image the IA translation and distortion can be seen; The IA on frame 1 is shown red and the IA on
frame 2 is shown yellow.
Right clicking an IA will open a context menu, from where the correlation image or the transformed
IA can be viewed by selecting the menu items ‘Show correlation image’ or ‘Show interrogation
areas’ respectively.
By default the particle image will be shown behind the interrogation areas and vectors, but the Inter-
rogation Areas can be hidden by removing the check mark in 'Show Int Areas', and the particle
image can be replaced by the Mask (if selected), by a map of estimated particle density or removed
completely by changing 'Image to show'.
PAGE|494
The image above shows the estimated particle density as an 8-bit image, where each pixel value
can be interpreted as the average number of particles within a 64x64 interrogation area centered
around the current location.
To detect particles a background estimate is first made and subtracted. From this the local noise
level is estimated and the image is divided by this noise to get an SNR-image. The SNR-image is
binarized with a user defined threshold (The parameter 'Particle detection limit' in the 'Adaptivity'
tab). Peaks in the resulting image are interpreted as particles and they are finally counted within
local neighborhoods and spatial smoothing applied to get a density map such as the one above.
The parameter 'Particle detection limit' in the 'Adaptivity' tab is the only recipe parameter that
affects the density map.
Since a grayscale peak is by definition brighter than all neighbors in a 3x3 neighborhood at most
25% of all pixels can be peaks(/particles). The max-value of 255 could thus theoretically be found
within a 32x32 neighborhood, but it is very unlikely in practice. For low particle densities this res-
olution is rather coarse, so we scale up by a factor of 4 and estimate particle count in 64x64 neigh-
borhoods even if this may theoretically lead to saturation (particle counts above 255 are truncated).
A change of any of the recipe settings will make the iteration start over when 'Step' is next pressed.
PAGE|495
16.6.6 Reference Vector Map
If/when approximate or expected particle displacements are known, Interrogation Areas on frame 1
& 2 are offset relative to one another. From the 2nd iteration and onward these offsets are naturally
based on results from the previous iteration, but in the first iteration particle displacements are typ-
ically not known and no offset can be applied. It is however possible to include a displacement pre-
dictor in the form of a reference vector map, from which offsets in the first iteration can be
determined. Such a reference vector map could e.g. be the result of an ensemble correlation on the
same image ensemble, providing a (temporal) average flow field. The reference vector map must
be preselected before entering the Adaptive PIV recipe ("Selection (Input to Analysis)" on
page 468). The reference vector map must have the same number of vectors in the same locations
as the ones generated by Adaptive PIV itself, otherwise it will be ignored.
16.7 Adaptive Shadow Tracking
The AdaptiveShadowTrackingmethod in DynamicStudio allows to detect and track particles
either from double-frame or single-frame shadow images. The method allows not only to detect
particles, but also to extract the contours and shape parameters. Additionally, a ShadowSizeCal-
ibrationcan be selected as an input, resulting in two main new results from the method: corrected
size and depth location of the detected particles.
Shadow imaging is suited to measure particle sizes and velocities in low to medium dense flows,
meaning that the occlusion between the particles is not high and an optical access is available to
provide direct back-light illumination. When these conditions are met, the method allows to extract
the particles' information without relying on other assumptions, like particle roundness, sphericity
and refraction index, like in Phase Doppler Anemometry (PDA) systems. However, the meas-
urement system is heavily dependent on image analysis and the computational resources to pro-
cess that information, requiring in many cases to analyze the information in post-processing, rather
than online as in PDA.
This document explains the base theory of shadow imaging and the solution implemented in
DynamicStudio. This is separated in following sections.
Summary:
l
Basic theory of shadow imaging and the out-of-focus challenge ["Theory" below].
l
User Interface (UI) in DynamicStudio together with all the parameters ["User interface
(UI)" on page 499].
l
Detection procedure step-by-step instructions ["Detection procedure and results" on
page 504].
l
Example walk through, from acquiring images to getting results ["Example" on
page 508].
16.7.1 Theory
Introduction
Shadow images are generated by placing an object directly in the line of sight between a light
source and a camera optics. The generated image is then the shadow of the object, and not the
object itself, like in normal photography using reflected/refracted light that bounces back to the cam-
era from the object. The schematic view shows the working principle in the figure below, where a
round particle of characteristic size
is placed at a distance
from the camera optics, in front of
PAGE|496
a backlight illumination source. The result shows that if the particle is not placed at the place where
the particle is in focus, the size and true shape of the particle is no longer reproduced correctly.
From this, the main challenges in shadow imaging for particles characterization are:
l
To accurately detect particle sizes and shape parameters. How to achieve this when
blurry objects are present in the image?
l
To track particles in time, either between frame pairs or in a time series.
l
Is there any measurement bias to consider?
The procedure presented in this guide will help to post-process images like the one below, where
polydispersed water droplets from a spray are seen at different sizes and shapes. So, the previous
points must be addressed first if accurate and precise size statistics are to be calculated.
Image thresholding and contours extraction
Thresholding of shadow images (or any image) means to select an intensity level i, usually given as
a value in the image depth in bits (e.g. 0-4095 in 12-bit space), and apply a given value to the pixels
above or below that threshold. In shadow images, particles are commonly seen as dark objects in
light background, so, selecting an adequate threshold to define a border for the particle would allow
to create a binary mask, from which particle properties can be extracted. The figure below shows
PAGE|497
this classical procedure, where a threshold level is chosen to create this binary mask, which con-
tains information about the size, shape and location of the particles.
However, a complication arises from this procedure, particles are not usually in-focus (close the to
the optics focal plane). In the figure below, the defocusing effect is shown for a particle image with a
(focused) diameter of 100 pixels. By acquiring the same image at different locations away from this
focal plane, it can be seen that it is not straightforward to select a given intensity level ito create the
binary mask and calculate the particle parameters. The particle size changes as a function of the
selected intensity level, and it is no longer 100 pixels in diameter. How to proceed then?
Global detection and local thresholding (with or without calibration input)
To solve this problem, a global-detection/local-thresholding algorithm is applied. This method
allows to detect particles that are in-focus and out-of-focus, eliminating the ambiguity of choosing a
threshold level, providing a robust method to calculate the true particle sizes with better accuracy
and precision.
Some definitions about the parameters and calculated quantities for a given particle:
l
Local pixel intensity,
: intensity value in a bounding box surrounding the particle.
l
Maximum local intensity,
: Maximum intensity value in the particle bounding box.
l
Minimum local intensity,
: Minimum intensity value in the particle bounding box.
l
Local image contrast:
l
Maximum contrast,
: Maximum contrast ratio of all the particles.
PAGE|498
l
Contrast coefficient:
l
Normalized contrast:
, the same contrast but normalized with
respect to the darkest possible particle, with
l
Threshold level,
: intensity level at a given percentage between
and
.
l
Equivalent diameter (pix):
, with A the projected surface area in pixels
of the contour at
.
The global-detection / local-thresholding algorithm can be summarized as follows:
1.
A global detection is used to calculate the rough particles' positions and bounding
boxes. Three methods are proposed for this, being the last one the best overall solu-
tion to detect out-of-focus particles:
1.
Global Threshold: Classic threshold at a given percentage between
and
.
2.
Otsu Threshold: Histogram based threshold based on the Otsu's method.
3.
Adaptive Threshold: Local patches Gaussian-wighted variable thresholding.
2.
Using the bounding boxes of the global detection, the local contrast ratio is calculated
in the local vicinity of the particle, if it is too blurry it is rejected and eliminated from the
analysis.
3.
Within the detected bounding box, a local threshold is applied at a given level. This
threshold is then used to calculate the particle properties: centroid, equivalent dia-
meter, surface area, minor-major axes, etc. This is an important step in the detection,
because by construction a threshold level always exist between
and
. The
local threshold is then explicitly defined as:
.
4.
If a Shadow Calibration is used, two more local threshold levels are extracted, and the
compensated diameter is estimated and the particle z-depth position (see Shadow
SizeCalibrationfor more details).
5.
Repeat 1-4 for the next frame, and find the matching particle to build a track. This gives
the velocity calculation and tracking in time-resolved or double-frame images.
Tracking of particles and velocity calculation
The particle tracking from shadow images rely only on the centroids. It is assumed that the centroid
positions between frames change in a "logical way". As particles flow through the image frame,
they follow a main preferred direction, allowing to guess the particle position in the next frame.
Then, if a centroid is located in this new "probable location" the particle is associated with the pre-
vious one to form a match. Providing that the time between frames and the scale factor (in mm/pix)
are known, then the velocity is calculated from the displacement between the centroids.
To increase the reliability of the matching an iterative method is considered, where an estimation of
the "probable position" in the next frame is calculated on-the-fly using the following methodology:
1.
A given user-input average displacement is used to find the centroids in the second
frame at a given radius from the position in the first one. Therefore, a constant dis-
placement field is used to guess the probable particle locations for matching in every
PAGE|499
particle.
2.
The firstly found and validated particles matches are used to build a Voronoi field with
the displacement as its main value, allowing to update the guess for the particle loc-
ations locally. This is based on the assumption that locally in the image, particles follow
each other in the same direction.
3.
Steps 1-2 are repeated a number of times in an iterative process.
This algorithm is then a refinement of the global constant displacement guess, taking the same
reasoning to the local vicinity of the matches particles, increasing the accuracy of the particle
matching. If time-resolved single-frame images are used, a track is build based on the previous
known locations. Similarly, if double-frame images are acquired, then the track only last for a pair of
frames, but the overall iterative calculation process applies.
16.7.2 User interface (UI)
The provided user interface of the method allows to perform the following tasks:
1.
Fine tune some key parameters to optimize the particles' detection.
2.
Introduce filters to discard or include certain types of detected objects or particles.
3.
Provide visual clues about the overall detection performance in a live window.
4.
Provide the initial input for the matching-tracking algorithm.
The description of the UI is detailed next.
General overview
The general overview of the UI for the Adaptive Shadow Tracking method is shown in the figure
below. It contains in the left side (red area) 3 tabs with the settings, and in the right side the Particle
View, where graphical cues about the particle detection are shown.
Settings
[red section above]
PAGE|500
The settings section is sub-divided in the 3 tabs, seen always in the upper part of the area. Each tab
goes deeper in complexity level, leaving the most necessary parameters in the first tab only.
These are: Processing, Advanced and Filters & Tracking.
Processing
The processing section allows to do a pre-processing of the images and select the detection
method to find the particles.
Pre processing
The Pre-processing introduces an option run a non-local means denoising algorithm on-the-fly.
The strength of the filter is the only parameter to choose. This is useful when random Gaussian
noise is present in the images, producing bad contours and shape/size information from the
particles.
The main advantage, compared to other denoising algorithms is that it keeps the shape and gradi-
ents of the image almost intact, avoiding the introduction of blur information like other methods
(Gaussian, mean, median filters). This is particularly useful to accurately extract the shape inform-
ation and true sizes of particles.
Note:Althoughthisdenoisingmethodperformsremarkablygood,theusermustbewarnedthatit
takesalongtimetocompute(severalsecondsperimage),addingasignificantamountofcom-
putingtime.
The menu UI to enable the denoising method and the strength is shown below, together with a pair
of images showing the performance of the algorithm.
Without denoising:
With denoising:
PAGE|501
Detection method
Three detection methods are proposed to find the global particles' positions in the image. As pre-
viously mentioned, the actual contours and particle properties are extracted later on in a local ana-
lysis. These methods are:
1.
Global threshold: In this mode, a simple global threshold is applied at a given per-
centage from the
and
on the global image. The result of this method can
be seen in the following figure, where a 50% threshold was selected and every particle
in the frame is detected using this value. Once the particles are found and validated,
the local thresholds are applied in the bounding boxes shown.
2.
Otsu threshold: In this mode, the Otsu's optimal threshold is calculated between the
and
on the global image, resulting effectively in a single global threshold
as in the previous case. The result of this method can be seen in the following figure,
where a threshold to the 100% of the calculated Otsu's one was selected and every
particle in the frame is detected using this value. Once the particles are found and val-
idated, the local thresholds are applied in the bounding boxes shown.
PAGE|502
3.
Local Adaptive: In this mode, particles are detected using a local adaptive window.
Sliding this window through the image allows to define different threshold levels, which
is important in situations where particles with large difference in contrast ratio are
present. The settings in the adaptive threshold method are the interrogationwindow
sizeand a thresholdmodifier. In the first one, setting up a large window benefits large
particles, being less sensitive in the small range. The modifier is used to balance the
local threshold towards more sharp particles or blurred particles. The effect of this can
be visualized immediately in the Particle View.
Advanced
The advanced tab shows the actual local contour levels to extract for the local analysis inside of the
bounding boxes. Three contour are extracted:
l
Threshold level, In green: this is the main threshold level, where the particle properties
and size are calculated. It is also used together with the ShadowSizeCalibrationto cal-
culate the Compensated Diameter in the results.
PAGE|503
l
Upper level for gradient, in purple: Used to calculate the particle edge gradient
together with the ShadowSizeCalibration.
l
Lower level for gradient, in yellow: Used to calculate the particle edge gradient
together with the ShadowSizeCalibration.
These contours are displayed in the Particle View as equivalent surface diameters, with the same
color codes .
The blue square is the bounding box. Ideally it should be set so that the thresholds sit inside of the
local particle area. This is shown all together in the following figure.

Filters & Tracking
Filter Settings
The filter settings allow to discard from the analysis any given particle too eccentric or non-round,
this can be done by moving the Eccentricity and Roundness handles. A minimum and maximum
particle size are (in pixels) can be defined, the particle view shows the equivalent diameter in
l
Particle Eccentricity: The analysis method will reject particles that are more eccentric
than user-selected ratio MaxAxis / MinAxis. If the particle is perfect round, then the
ratio MaxAxis/MinAxis will be one.
l
Particle roundness (Normalized Particle Momentum of Inertia): The analysis method
will set up a validation criteria based on particle's roundness. This parameter is also
called normalized momentum of inertial, made to measure the spread of points around
the center of mass.
PAGE|504
Tracking
The tracking of particles between frames can be activated in this section. As previously shown, the
initial search radius must be entered, together with the maximum number of iteration steps.
To provide a good input for this initial parameter, switching between Frame1/Frame2 might be use-
ful to see the typical displacement of the particles (if in double-frame) or to switch to another frame
using the Image/FrameSettingsslider.
Particle View
The particle view allows to visualize in real time the consequence of the chosen settings above.
This is shown in each of the previous sub-sections.
16.7.3 Detection procedure and results
The following procedure is recommended for spray particles, as show throughout this doc-
umentation. This also includes the application of the ShadowSizeCalibration.
Detection
Processing tab:
PAGE|505
1.
Select a "Shadow Size Calibration" output method.
2.
Select the image ensemble to process (shadow images in single or double-frame).
3.
Under Particle Characterization, select Adaptive Shadow Tracking.
4.
Select if you would like to only process the displayed ROI in the Particle View.
5.
Choose if you would like to pre-process the images or not. A Non-Local Means Denois-
ing method is available.
6.
Select the particle detection method: Global Threshold, Otsu Based Threshold, Local
Adaptive. Local Adaptive gives the best results in many scenarios, the Interrogation
Window Size (order of magnitude of the particle sizes, 243 in the example) and the
Threshold Modifier (positive value for dark particles in white background, 4 in the
example) can be adjusted.
Advanced Tab:
1.
Specify the bounding box around the detected particles to extract the contours. 150%
should cover enough of the defocused particles as shown in the example. You can
choose to draw the bounding box in the UI.
2.
Specify the contour levels to extract. The particle will be measured using the Threshold
Level (in green). The calibration defocusing function will be calculated form the high
(purple) and low (yellow) thresholds levels. Ideally the same threshold levels from the
"Shadow Size Calibration" should be selected. You can choose to draw the equivalent
diameters from the contours in the UI.
3.
Check that the contours are properly drawn inside of the bounding boxes.
Filters & Tracking tab:
1.
Filters are available to quickly eliminate from the analysis particles with large eccent-
ricity, poor roundness or area outside a specified minimum-maximum range (in pixels)
.
2.
Activating tracking will match particles from the double-frame images in the example,
the Initial Search Radius is given (in pixels), as the average displacement between
frames for the objects (80 pixels in this case). Iteration steps are used to estimate a
Voronoi velocity area field, that will adapt the probable search area to the one recal-
culated in the given iterations (Maximum of 5 iteration steps are enough in this case).
Results
The results of the Adaptive Shadow Tracking is the particles contour and the calculated properties
from it, together with the particle velocity if double-frame or time-resolved tracking is enabled.

Equivalent Diameter is the diameter of the spherical particle having the same projected area sur-
face as the measured object. This is calculated from the Threshold Level contour.
Area is the area of the measured object calculated from the Threshold Level contour.
Perimeter is the perimeter the measured object
Eccentricity expresses the elongation of the object. For a circle, Ecc is 1 and for a line it is greater
than one. The eccentricity is defined as

where Pis the perimeter and Sthe projected area of the shape.
Major and Minor axis length are the major and minor radii of the ellipsis calculated as
,
U and V velocity are defined along the horizontal and vertical axis respectively.
Equivalent volume are calculated assuming the volume of a sphere from the Equivalent Dia-
meters.
Speed is the magnitude of velocity
