If field Output.type is not present then the output dataset's type is determined from field names:
l
image when 'frame1' is found
l 3D vectors if U and V and W
l 2D vectors if U and V, but not W
l 1D vectors (Scalars) if only U or S
l otherwise a generic column based dataset is created
Output.dataset
Like Input{1}.dataset Output.dataset can contain a single dataset or be an array of datasets that will
then be part of an output ensemble. The simplest processing will transfer a single dataset to
MATLAB and likewise return a single dataset to DynamicStudio. This way datasets can be pro-
cessed one by one. It is also possible to transfer an entire ensemble of datasets to MATLAB and
return a single dataset such as a mean or similar statistic. For other types of analyses it is possible
to transfer an entire data ensemble to MATLAB and get an entire ensemble back. The two
ensembles need not necessarily have the same number of datasets. If datasets are transferred to
MATLAB one at a time, but each MATLAB session returns multiple datasets the final ensemble will
contain all the various outputs sequentially. (This is possible, but not recommended).
Image Map output
Allowed fields in Output.dataset are frame1 and frame2. Must be 2D arrays of int8, uint8, int16,
uint16 or double. Image width and height are deducted from the dimensions of frame1:
image.width = GetN(frame1)
image.height = GetM(frame1)
Verification of frame2 dimensions is performed and user notified on mismatch.
If Output.pixelDepth is not present pixelDepth is set to 8, 16 or 64 according to array type.
Scalar or Vector Map output
Allowed fields in Output.dataset are U,V,W,S, and status or Status. Must be 2D arrays of int32,
uint32, single, or double. Grid dimensions are deducted from U (or S) and all other fields are veri-
fied against the array dimensions of U (or S).
PAGE|706
Generic output
All fields in Output.dataset will result in a column of the same name and type. Must be 1D arrays of
int32, uint32, single, or double. Both vertical (Mx1) and horizontal (1xN) 1D arrays are allowed, but
2D arrays are not supported (you can of course return such arrays column by column). Columns do
not need to be of same length.
From within DynamicStudio such a generic column dataset can be displayed numerically or using a
XY-plot (See "XY-Plot " (on page 1074)). Double-click the resulting display to choose which values
to show on which axis.
If the XY-plot does not suffice you can also use MATLABs built-in visualization tools to generate a
figure and save a screendump of such a figure in the DynamicStudio database along with the
numerical values: An optional metafile is supported to save a copy of a MATLAB figure if a figure
window is open at the time data is returned to DynamicStudio. Multiple figures can only be returned
if they are shown in the same display window. If multiple figure windows are open only the most
recently accessed will be transferred.
Generic output - metafile only
When Output.type = figure, picture or metafile a column dataset is created with zero columns, but a
metafile with a copy of figure window contents can be transferred.
16.55 Troubleshooting, Tips & Tricks
Connecting DynamicStudio & MATLAB
In general the installation of DynamicStudio should be postponed until MATLAB has been installed,
run, closed again and the PC restarted. In most cases this will ensure that DynamicStudio can find
MATLAB once installed.
If DynamicStudio cannot find MATLAB or fail to connect to it, MATLAB may not appear in the Win-
dows registry database as a COM server. Try reinstalling MATLAB and restarting the PC. If this
does not help you can register MATLAB manually, by opening a Command Prompt Window as an
administrator:
Click Start -> All Programs -> Accessories -> Right-Click 'Command Prompt' and select 'Run as
administrator':
PAGE|707
Answer 'Yes' when the User Account Control prompts you to confirm that 'Windows Command Pro-
cessor' should be allowed to make changes to the computer.
In the resulting command window type 'matlab -regserver' and press Enter:
MATLAB should now start with just the command window, and the Command Prompt Window
above can be closed.
DynamicStudio should now be able to find and connect with MATLAB, if not you may have to
restart the computer once more.
Alternatively you can register MATLAB as an Automation Server from the MATLAB Command Win-
dow instead of the Windows Command Window described above;
This requires that you start MATLAB with the 'Run as administrator' option :
PAGE|708
When MATLAB is up and running type ...
>> regmatlabserver
...and press enter.
If you started MATLAB normally (i.e. not 'Run as Administrator') you will get an error message if you
try this.
Otherwise you can now close MATLAB, restart the PC and power up MATLAB and DynamicStudio
normally.
When DynamicStudio has established connection with MATLAB once, it should be able to do so
again without using the Windows Command Prompt, so you need to register only once.
Running DynamicStudio and MATLAB on 64-bit platforms
On a 64-bit hardware platform running 64-bit Windows, MATLAB will by default be installed as a
64-bit application. DynamicStudio 4.00 and onward are 64-bit applications also, but DynamicStudio
3.41 and older are 32-bit applications. They will run smoothly on a 64-bit platform, but be unable to
connect with 64-bit applications, meaning that the link between the two programs cannot be estab-
lished.
PAGE|709
To overcome this problem you may install MATLAB as a 32-bit application, with which Dynam-
icStudio should have no problems establishing a connection. According to MathWorks, the sup-
pliers of MATLAB, 32-bit MATLAB on a 64-bit platform is not officially supported, but should work
for most users:
Quote (Solution ID 1-1CAT7): ...
"SinceMathWorksoffers64-bitMATLABbinariesforbothWindowsandLinux,wedo
notsupportrunning32-bitMATLABbinarieson64-bitWindowsorLinuxarchitecture.
However,eventhoughwedonotofficiallysupporttheseconfigurations,installingand
running32-bitMATLABon64-bitWindowsorLinuxmachinesisexpectedtoworkfor
mostusers."
...endquote.
User interface of MATLAB
For historical reasons and compatibility with older versions, MATLAB will by default be launched
with a simple command window when started from inside DynamicStudio. With later versions of
MATLAB a graphical user interface was introduced and it can in fact be set up to accept con-
nections from other software such as DynamicStudio. It requires that you start up
MATLAB manually and enter the following command:
>> enableservice('AutomationServer',true);
-if you use the MATLAB-Link frequently you may choose to add this command to the script star-
tup.m, which will be executed automatically every time MATLAB is started. If you start
MATLAB before attempting to use the link and enable the automation server as described above,
DynamicStudio will establish a link with the running version of MATLAB instead of opening a new
command window. Once data has been transferred from DynamicStudio to MATLAB you can open,
edit and display variables from inside the graphical user interface and run or debug scripts using
MATLAB's integrated script editor.
16.56 1. Mie-LIF SMD - General
This analysis method comes from older DynamicStudio versions, due to a lack of test-
ing data we could not verify the correct behavior of this method. If you encounter
issues with the analysis method please contact the support, or use the bug report fea-
ture to raise a ticket in the feedback under the help tab
Both SMD Calibration and Process require a data structure as below:
Project
---Calibration
---Camera1#(CalibrationTarget)
---Camera2#(CalibrationTarget)
---Camera1#(Dye=ON)
---Camera2#(Dye=ON)
---Camera1#(Dye=OFF)
---Camera2#(Dye=OFF)
---RunZ=Zmin
---Camera1#
---Camera2#
---RunZ=Zmin+Zstep
---Camera1#
PAGE|710
---Camera2#
…
---RunZ=Zmax
---Camera1#
---Camera2#
Please refer the Reference and User Guide of Mie/LIF SMD measurement for details regarding
how to acquire images.
NOTE:
The calibration method and SMD process expect that camera 1# is the LIF camera and camera 2#
is the Mie camera. This must not be changed by the user.
To determine the quantitative SMD a PDA is used as a reference to correct for any serious devi-
ations. Diameter and SMD data are collected over a 3D traverse grid enclosing the spray of
interest. The mathematical model used to fit the PDA data to the SMD imaged result is:
(a)
where :
X = Reference SMD (PDA data)
Y= Mie-Lif ratio
γ= Absorption
a0, a1, α ,ß , and are the coefficients to be determined.
The coefficients are determined by calculating over a range of those four parameters mentioned
above, using linear regression then and determining the values that give the smallest residues.
The absorption due to the spray between the measurement plane and the camera is also con-
sidered in the SMD calibration and process, and will be calculated by comparing the signal
acquired at different ‘Z’ positions.
In DynamicStudio, the dataset is first calibrated by SMD calibration to determine the parameters
shown in Equ. (a). Once the parameters are calculated, the SMD calibration results can be applied
to the dataset to generate a 2D SMD distribution map by the SMD Process.
16.57 2. SMD Calibration
The typical input data is introduced in section 1, please follow the procedure below to find the right
coeffients in Equ. (a)
1. Select all the dewarped images as input in Dynamic Studio (Fig. 1, left), including background
images with and without dye as well as spray images at acquired at all ‘Z’ position.
2. Right click one of images residing in the ‘Calibration’ category, select ‘Calibrations’ and enter the
interface to select ‘SMD Calibration’, as shown in Fig. 2 (right).
PAGE|711
Figure 1 Select ‘SMD Calibration’
3. Press ‘OK’ and a recipe dialog will be displayed consisting of six tabbed pages of input:
· Mie optical setup
PAGE|712
Figure 2 Mie optical Setup in SMD Calibration
The Mie optical setup (shown in Fig. 2) contains the efficiencies for all the components related to
the transmission of laser light into the measurement volume for the Mie process. The user can
select the efficiencies for the mirror, beam splitter, laser and windows. If the image is a double
imaged the user can select which image is relevant. Any component that is missing or deactivated
is simply marked with unity (1.0).
· LIF optical setup
PAGE|713
Figure 3 LIF optical setup in SMD Calibration
The LIF optical setup (shown in Fig. 3) is identical to the Mie setup and in most setups it will have
indentical parameters.
· SMD setup
PAGE|714
Figure 4 SMD setup in SMD Calibration
The SMD setup (shown in Fig. 4) contains four adjustment parameters that affect the filtering of the
final SMD result:
►Mie coefficient: low pass filter based on the percentage of the Mie mean image value – mainly to
remove strong reflection.
►LIF coefficient: low pass filter based on the percentage of the LIF mean image value – mainly to
remove strong reflection as well.
►Minimum SMD: the minimum acceptable SMD value.
►Maximum SMD: the maximum acceptable SMD value.
· PDA input setup
PAGE|715
Figure 5 PDA setup in SMD Calibration
The PDA input setup (shown in Fig. 5) assists in the loading of one or more PDA statistics datasets.
The user can select the amount of acceptable deviation in position and the assignment of coordin-
ate axes. The user can either press ‘Add’ button to import data from .lda project (data file acquired
from Dantec Dynamics PDA system), or press ‘Import’ to import data from .txt or .xls file.
Note
If the PDA results are imported from text or excel file, it should contain data needed as the same lay-
out; no headline is allowed in this file.
· Coordinate setup
PAGE|716
Figure 6 Coordinates setup in SMD Calibration
In this page (shown in Fig. 6) the user needs to define the relation between the coordinates used for
imaging and the one used for PDA measurement. The user can also define where the nozzle is loc-
ated in the acquired images.
Once everything is defined, click ‘Apply’, the SMD calibration will be performed. The calibration res-
ults can be displayed either as Fig. 7 or as numerical (Fig. 8), which will also show the value of
those coefficients in Equ. (a).
PAGE|717

SMD ref: SMD results measured by PDA;
SMD Mie/LIF: SMD results measured by Mie/LIF (without calibration);
A0, A1, α, β: correspond to parameters shown in Equ. (a) – They are displayed as identical for all
points;

16.58 3. SMD Process
Once the SMD calibration is performed, the calibration results can be applied on spray images with
similar conditions (mainly regarding ambient pressure, ambient temperature and spray optical dens-
ity) by SMD processing, which will provide the SMD 2D distribution in the end.
Similarly to the calibration the user is required to select the dewarped background images (both
Dye ON and Dye OFF) for all cameras, dewarped spray images at all ‘Z’ position and the calibration
PAGE|718
results. Right click one of the selected image residing within the ‘Run’, and select ‘Analyze’; the
interface to select Analysis Method will pop up, as shown in Fig. 9.

‘Select Analysis Method’ interface.

ilar as the interface of SMD Calibration:

16.59 Modal Analysis (POD, sPOD, mPOD & FMD)
Modal Analysis offer four different decompositions of data series
l Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (Classic POD)
l Spectral Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (sPOD)
l Multi-Scale Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (mPOD)
l Fourier Mode Decomposition (FMD)
Pleasenote:Thelastthreearedongleprotectedandmakessensefortime-resolvedinputonly!
Decompositionsnotsupportedbythedonglewillbevisibleintherecipebutgreyedoutsotheuser
cannotselectthem.
IfdatafromasPOD,mPODorFMDanalysisisavailablebutthecurrentdongledoesnotsupportit,
PAGE|723
thedatacanbedisplayedandused,but'ShowRecipe'or'PerformAnalysisagain'willrevertback
toClassicPOD.
All four analyses decompose a 'signal' that varies in space and time into a series of 'modes', each
with a scalar amplitude λ, a spatial distribution ϕ(x)and a temporal evolution ψ(t).
Combining these modes the original signal can be reconstructed:
= Global amplitude of the n'th mode
= n'th Spatial mode, 'Topos'
= n'th Temporal mode, 'Chronos'
The square of the modal amplitudes describes modal 'energy' and modes are sorted by descending
amplitude/energy, so the dominant modes are listed first.
The Topos describes spatial variations and are independent of time.
The Chronos describe how each of the modes contribute to the reconstruction as a function of time.
These are independent of spatial locations.
By design the Chronos are mutually orthogonal so the dot product of a Chronos with itself is 1,
while the dot product of two different Chronos is 0:
For Classic POD the Topos are mutually orthogonal as well:
The modes may themselves provide insights into the data investigated and decompositions are
often used for reduced order modeling (ROM), since an excellent approximation to the original sig-
nal can be made from just a few of the first (high-energy) modes. The first modes typically describe
large scale coherent structures, while the last modes often describe only noise.
Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (POD) was first introduced in the context of Fluid Mechanics by
Lumley in 1967 1 . It is closely related to Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) and Principal Com-
ponent Analysis (PCA) from linear algebra. The original POD analysis will find spatial modes only,
but adding the temporal modes also is straightforward. For classic POD both spatial and temporal
modes are orthogonal, so Aubry et al 2called it Bi-Orthogonal Decomposition (BOD) and also
coined the names 'Topos' and 'Chronos' for spatial and temporal modes respectively. There are
many other names for the technique, but the popular 'POD' is used here, while the names 'Topos'
and 'Chronos' from Aubry et al are adopted.
Classic POD is energy optimal in the sense that each new mode added recovers as much as pos-
sible of the energy from the original data. But all POD Chronos are broadband in the frequency
domain and may include signal content all the way from DC to the Nyquist limit (half the sample rate
of the original data). Fourier Mode Decomposition (FMD), as described by e.g. Ma et al in 20151, is
1Ma, L., Feng, L., Pan, C. et al. (2015):
Fourier mode decomposition of PIV data.
Sci. China Technol. Sci. 58, 1935–1948, 19 August 2015.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11431-015-5908-y
PAGE|724
spectrally pure so each Chronos has one specific frequency, but it is not optimal in terms of modal
energy distribution. As a compromise between energy optimality and spectral purity Sieber et al pro-
posed Spectral POD (sPOD) in 2016 1 and Mendez et al proposed Multi-Scale POD (mPOD) in
20193,4.
Both sPOD, mPOD and FMD has been incorporated into DynamicStudio as possible alternatives to
Classic POD.
To perform a complete or partial reconstruction from an existing Modal Analysis, please see "Modal
Reconstruction / Projection" on page 757.
16.59.1 Supported input
Most of the following examples assume that u represent (2C) velocity vectors from a time series of
planar (2D) vector maps. But the input data can be any time series of vectors or scalars in a planar
or volumetric spatial domain, so it could also be e.g. Volumetric PIV (3D3C), Stereo-PIV (2D3C),
scalar maps (2D1C) or pixel values from an image. Scalar maps can represent any physical quant-
ity, while velocity vectors are the only multivariate input with a dedicated display.
Modal Analysis can handle time series with up to 16000 datasets.
The Topos will inherit the type of the parent data, so if input data are vector maps, Topos will be vec-
tor maps also, if Scalar Maps are provided as input the Topos will also become scalar maps and if
input is images, Topos will become images as well.
Amplitude, Chronos and other properties derived from those are the same no matter what kind of
input is used.
16.59.2 Recipe & settings
Select an input ensemble of images, scalar or vector maps and select analysis method 'Modal Ana-
lysis':
1Sieber, Paschereit & Oberleithner (2016):
Spectral proper orthogonal decomposition
Journal of Fluid Mechanics, Volume 792, pp. 798-828, March 2016
PAGE|725
16.59.3 Modal Analysis recipe
Input data will start loading as soon as the recipe opens as shown by a progress bar at the bottom
of the recipe. The data in each dataset will be organized in a so-called 'Snapshot' vector and a
Covariance matrix is built up by computing the dot product between such snapshot vectors from the
different timesteps (datasets). The main diagonal of the Covariance matrix holds the dot product of
each snapshot vector with itself and right next to those are the dot product of temporal neighbors. N
datasets will create N snapshot vectors and a symmetric NxN Covariance matrix. While filling in the
Covariance matrix as many snapshots as possible are stored in RAM, but with many and/or large
datasets there may not be room for them all in which case some of the datasets will have to be
loaded from disk several times. For every N'th of the Covariance values filled in the progress bar is
updated and a preliminary 2D Fourier Transform computed from the present Covariance matrix
(with zeros for the values not yet filled in). From the complex FFT a real power spectrum is com-
puted and all energy mirrored into the 1st quadrant for display in the recipe as an image. Greyscale
values follow a logarithmic scale so negative values are common even if this is a power spectrum.
Recipe with default settings is shown below:
Frequencies on the horizontal and vertical axes are relative to the sample rate of the original data
with DC (0,0) in the lower left corner and the Nyquist limit in the top right at half the sample rate of
the original data.
Below the 2D frequency spectrum a 1D spectrum shows the values along the diagonal of the 2D
spectrum above. Frequency 0 (DC) is to the left and the Nyquist limit to the right. Color scale in the
2D spectrum and y-axis on the 1D spectrum are both logarithmic.
In the left hand side a list shows the parameters available in the parent datasets and the user can
choose which of them to decompose. For vector maps the default is (U,V,W), for other datatypes it
is the first available scalar. At least one parameter must remain selected at all times, if multiple para-
meters are selected, they must share a common unit. Beyond these simple rules it is the users own
responsibility if it makes sense to combine the parameters chosen. It is possible, but not recom-
mended to mix parameters that are mutually dependent. For example VorticityZ = dV/dX - dU/dY.
These three parameters are all available in the figure above and share a common unit, so it is pos-
sible to combine them into one analysis, but since they are mutually dependent, it will make little
sense to do so.
PAGE|726
Whenever a parameter in the list is selected or unselected, the computation of Covariance matrix
will start over and the progress bar at the bottom of the recipe jump back to the far left side.
Having decided which parameters to analyze the rest of the recipe is used to determine how the
chosen parameters are to be analyzed. Remaining recipe screendumps in this help file have been
cropped to focus on this part.
In the top left corner of the recipe choose whether to perform classic snapshot POD (the default),
Spectral sPOD, Multi-Scale mPOD or Fourier Mode FMD. Just below choose whether to include or
exclude the DC.
The differences between POD, mPOD, sPOD & FMD lie in what masking/weighting (if any) is
applied to the frequency domain Covariance matrix before it is transformed back to the temporal
domain for eigen-decomposition:
The sketches above illustrate the various weightings in the 1st quadrant of the frequency domain,
so DC is in the lower left corner and the Nyquist limit in the upper right. Weights are always sym-
metrical around the diagonal, which is always preserved and when applied the mask is mirrored to
the other three quadrants of the frequency domain. Both mPOD & FMD rely on frequency domain
masking, where all frequency components are multiplied with either one or zero. Spectral POD on
the other hand rely on frequency domain filtering, where all frequency components are multiplied
by a weight between one and zero.
Note that for FMD analysis it makes no difference if DC is included or excluded.
After frequency domain masking / filtering the Covariance matrix is transformed back to the tem-
poral domain and eigen-decomposition gives us the modal amplitudes (eigenvalues) and cor-
responding Chronos (eigenvectors). The Topos are subsequently computed by projecting the
original data onto the Chronos found.
16.59.4 Handling the Mean
In earlier versions of the POD Analysis the temporal mean was subtracted from the data before per-
forming the actual computation on just the fluctuating part. The mean was subsequently appended
to the results as 'Mode 0' with its own Amplitude, Topos and Chronos. This is now optional and, if
chosen, performed in the frequency domain. Unlike other frequency domain masking removal of
the DC (leftmost column & bottom row) is not shown graphically in the recipe. Within round-off error
excluding the DC via frequency domain masking gives the same results as before.
Including the mean makes sense especially when investigating non-stationary or transient phe-
nomena, otherwise excluding it is more in line with traditional Reynolds decomposition of a tur-
bulent flow into a stationary (DC) and a fluctuating part.
PAGE|727
16.59.5 Handling Outliers and Masked out data
While building up the Covariance matrix Modal Analysis will check the status codes of input data-
sets and accept both Valid and Substituted data. Masked out or Rejected data (i.e. known Outliers)
will however be excluded from the analysis. Masking is normally not a problem since a (Static)
mask will have accepted or rejected data from a specific location the same way for all datasets in
the parent ensemble, so each location will have either all valid data or no data at all. Dynamic Mask-
ing or Outliers that have been detected, but not substituted, does however present a problem since
data from these locations will be gappy, providing information for some, but not all of the timesteps
in the parent ensemble. Modal Analysis will attempt to handle the situation by averaging over the
number of valid data samples from each location, but due to the missing data it is likely to get fewer
modes than normal and an exact reconstruction of the parent datasets is no longer possible. For a
modest number of outliers in the parent ensemble reconstruction errors will however be small and a
reasonable approximation should still be possible.
To avoid this problem it is recommended to make sure that the input datasets contain no known out-
liers before applying the POD Analysis.
"Universal Outlier Detection" on page 936 can for example be used to substitute outliers with a reas-
onable estimate based on valid neighbors.
If unsubstituted outliers are found among the input data a warning will be shown in the notification
area in the lower left part of the recipe, saying that
'Input data contain outliers! '
This is a warning that an exact reconstruction will no longer be possible. Depending on the number
of outliers the effective number of modes may be reduced and the ones found may be more noisy
than otherwise. It is however possible to proceed with the analysis and in many cases Modal
Reconstruction will still create a reasonable approximation to the original data.
16.59.6 Time resolved Input
For classic POD it is not mandatory to have time resolved input, but without it the Chronos are of
little use. Spectral POD (sPOD), Multi-Scale POD (mPOD) and Fourier Mode Decomposition
(FMD) on the other hand makes no sense at all without time-resolved input and the frequency
domain filtering require that the time between samples is constant and positive. If they are not a
warning will appear in the notification area in the lower left part of the recipe:
'Time between snapshots not constant and positive! '
The warning will appear if the longest and shortest time between consecutive samples differ by
more than 5% of the average time between samples. This allows for small-scale timestamp jitter,
but catches larger errors.
It is possible to ignore the warning and proceed with the analysis, but for sPOD, mPOD and FMD
results may not be reliable since the assumption of fixed time between samples was violated.
16.59.7 Mode Count
Modal Analysis generates modes, that are stored as separate datasets in an ensemble. The num-
ber of modes will be reduced if the input data contain outliers, but will otherwise correspond to
either the number of datasets (snapshot count) in the parent ensemble or to the number of values
in each of those datasets (snapshot length), whichever of these numbers is the smallest. Imagine
for example an ensemble with 1000 vector maps, each with 40x30 2D vectors. The snapshot count
is of course 1000, while the snapshot length is 40x30x2=2400 (40x30 vectors, each with a U- and a
V-component). In this case modal analysis will create 1000 modes, corresponding to the 1000 snap-
shots in the parent ensemble. If the experiment is repeated with a longer time series and generate
5000 vector maps, still with 40x30 2D vectors in each, modal analysis will however not create 5000
modes, but just 40x30x2=2400.
PAGE|728
Most literature assume that the number of values in each dataset is (much) larger than the number
of datasets and this is by far the most common scenario.
In the opposite scenario a warning will appear in the notification are in the lower left part of the
recipe:
'Snapshot count exceed snapshot length! '
Analysis can proceed, but consider reprocessing the parent datasets to get more values in each
dataset. For vector maps the spacing between neighbor vectors could for example be reduced in
order to get more vectors in each vector map.
16.59.8 Multi-Scale POD (mPOD)
Choosíng Multi-Scale mPOD as Analysis Type in the upper left corner of the recipe the right hand
side of the recipe will be enabled allowing the frequency range from Zero to the Nyquist limit to be
divided into blocks:
In the example above the frequency range has been divided into 4 blocks with 3 cutoff frequencies
between the outer limits f*=0.0 and f*=0.5. All frequencies are relative to the sample rate of the par-
ent data so f*=0.5 corresponds to half the sample rate, i.e. the Nyquist limit. Sample rate is shown
along with sample count in the middle right side of the recipe for information. The vertical slider can
be used to browse frequencies or a desired value can be typed into the entry field at the top. Press-
ing the button 'Add Cutoff' will round the desired frequency to the nearest bin edge, add it to the list
of cutoff frequencies and show corresponding lines over the spectrum displays. To remove a cutoff
frequency select it in the list and click 'Clear Selected'. 'Clear All' will remove all but the outer limits
0.0 and 0.5 in order to start over. Performing an mPOD analysis with no cutoff frequencies is equi-
valent to performing a Classic POD analysis. It is not possible to edit cutoff frequencies already in
the list, only to remove old ones and add new.
Transition bandwidth / Tapering
So far the frequency domain masking has been described as 'brickwall' filters with sharp edges
jumping from a transmission of 0% to 100% and back again from one frequency bin to the next.
This may produce undesired ringing phenomena in the temporal domain, which can be reduced by
tapering the filter edges so the transition from 0% to 100% and back again is gradual over a number
PAGE|729
of frequency bins. This is controlled by the 'Transition bandwidth' in the lower right of the recipe.
The smallest nonzero transition bandwidth corresponds to 2 frequency bins and filter transmission
will then be 0%-25%-75%-100%. From there transition bandwidth can be increased in steps of 2 fre-
quency bins up to a maximum of 16 bins. To ensure that transmission will in fact reach 100% before
it starts going back down again, transition bandwidth is also limited to half the width of the smallest
of the frequency blocks chosen. Similarly any nonzero transition bandwidth will limit how close to
one another cutoff frequencies can be set. It is therefore recommended to leave the transmission
bandwidth at 0 (brick wall filters) until all cutoff frequencies have been set and then apply filter edge
tapering at the end.
Tapering is done exclusively in the passband of each filter in order to prevent overlap and thus
crosstalk between neighboring frequency bands.
No tapering is applied to edges adjacent to the DC, whether it is included or excluded.
16.59.9 Spectral POD (sPOD)
Choosing Spectral POD (sPOD) as Analysis Type will apply a filter on the frequency domain Covari-
ance matrix, preserving the diagonal, but assigning lower weights the further we move away from
the diagonal. The falloff is Gaussian and the user can specify the standard deviation Sigma* of that
Gaussian. Similar to the cutoff frequencies of mPOD shown above, the Sigma* is also specified as
relative to the sampling frequency of the parent data. It can be set from 0.001 to 10.0, the outermost
values being for all practical purposes FMD and POD respectively.
The filtering is visualized in the recipe and contour lines show where the filter applies weights of
0.99, 0.50 & 0.01 as shown in the figure above.
Please note that the sPOD filter is in fact applied along the frequency domain antidiagonal only, but
shown on the main diagonal here, since all 4 quadrants of the 2D frequency spectrum are mirrored
into the 1st quadrant anyway.
16.59.10 Fourier Mode Decomposition (FMD)
Choosing Fourier Mode Decomposition (FMD) as Analysis Type will simply mask out all but the
diagonal from the frequency domain Covariance matrix as illustrated in the recipe:
PAGE|730
Note that the DC handling has been grayed out since it makes no difference here; The DC is com-
pletely isolated just like all other frequencies.
16.59.11 Modal analysis
When 'OK' or 'Apply' is pressed the frequency domain representation of the Covariance matrix is
masked/filtered as illustrated in the recipe. After masking the Covariance matrix is transformed
back to the temporal domain and eigen-decomposition produces eigen-values and -vectors, which
in turn leads to Modal Amplitudes and Chronos. Corresponding Topos are finally determined by pro-
jection of the parent data onto the Chronos found.
Comparing results from Classic POD, Multi-Scale POD, Spectral POD & FMD the sample Topos
below show the same physical pattern:
PAGE|731
The last three are very similar, while the POD Topos in the top left is contaminated with structures
from other (lower) frequencies.
Looking at the corresponding Chronos and their Spectra the differences are clear:
PAGE|732
In all cases the green curve is the Chronos and the red one the corresponding Spectrum. The x-
axis is the frequency, which makes sense for the Spectrum only. (For better visibility we're showing
just the positive frequencies here and consequently crop the first half of the Chronos as well).
All spectra show a peak just above 70Hz, but the result from Classic POD is broadband including
signal at both higher and lower frequencies. In the mPOD results frequency content is restricted to
a narrow band around the peak. Where mPOD results contain no signal at all outside a specified
frequency band the sPOD Chronos shows a smoother falloff in frequency content. FMD results
finally go all the way and exclude all but the dominant frequency also visible in the first three.
Remaining frequency content is accounted for by other modes than the one shown here.
The following figure illustrate the effect of varying the frequency domain Sigma in Spectral POD
(sPOD).
From left to right the Sigma value decrease from 0.10 to 0.05, 0.02 and finally 0.01, where the first
one resemble results from classic POD, while the last one is closer to FMD results:
PAGE|733
In all cases we show a mode describing a vortex pattern associated with a dominant frequency in
the flow just above 70Hz.
The top row show the Topos, which is clearly the same vortex structure in all cases.
The bottom row shows the corresponding Chronos (green) and corresponding Spectra (red) and
here the differences show clearly;
The leftmost example has frequency content all the way from 0 to Nyquist (but suppresed com-
pared to corresponding results from classic POD). Going from left to right the frequency bandwidth
of the Chronos narrows down, suppressing signal content at frequencies above or below the dom-
inant peak.
Signal content at higher or lower frequencies is accounted for by other modes.
As with FMD (Fourier Mode Decomposition) the price we pay for spectral purity is a slower con-
vergence when reconstructing data from the modes. POD has the fastest possible convergence,
FMD the slowest and sPOD and mPOD are somewhere in between. This can be visualized by look-
ing at the Residual Energy Distribution as a function of mode number as shown below:
PAGE|734
With a residual of 1% we have recovered 99% of the fluctuating energy. In the example above POD
needs 150 modes to achieve this, while FMD requires 260 of the 420 modes available. Multiscale
and Spectral POD are all somewhere in between.
16.59.12 Mode Normalization / Scaling
As explained above, Chronos and (for POD) also Topos are by default orthonormal, meaning that
not only are the individual modes normal to one another, they also have unit length.
For the Topos this means that by default the Root (of the) Sum (of the) Squares of all displacement
components from all vectors will equal one. Similarly the Root (of the) Sum (of the) Squares of all
Chronos values should also equal one. From a mathematical point of view this is a perfectly normal
approach and corresponds to the default Mode Normalization 'Root Sum Square' in the recipe
above.
This approach however means also that the magnitude of individual vectors in each Topos will
depend on the total number of vectors and similarly the Chronos-values will depend on the number
of timesteps (/snapshots) included in the analysis. This makes it difficult to compare results from dif-
ferent analyses, so Mode Normalization may be set to 'Root Mean Square' instead. This causes
both Topos and Chronos to be scaled up so the Root (of the) Mean (of the) Squares of all elements
equals one. In effect this means that the average Topos vector has a length of one no matter how
many vectors there are and similarly the Chronos values will have an average magnitude of one no
matter how many snapshots/timesteps were included in the analysis.
To make sure the fundamental decomposition remains valid, the modal amplitudes are scaled
down, when corresponding Topos and Chronos are scaled up. Examples are shown below.
Please Note: For velocity vectors the normalization apply to the raw pixel displacements, not to the
metric velocities derived from them and shown as default by the vectormap display. Similar con-
siderations apply to scalar maps such as vorticity, derived from vector maps. In the examples
below the vector map display has been set to show pixel displacements instead of metric velocities.
PAGE|735
Mode 0 will usually be the mean of the input datasets followed by the actual POD modes sorted by
descending amplitude/energy.
The default display of a POD mode is the Topos, inheriting the datatype of the parent datasets;
When input is a series of vector maps, all Topos will be vector maps also.
PAGE|736
The Topos above are identical apart from the normalization. The first figure shows RSS-Scaled
Topos, the second figure RMS-Scaled Topos. The two are topologically identical, but note the dif-
ferent numerical values in the colorbar at the bottom.
The following examples show what Topos may look like when POD is applied to Scalar and Image
maps (both are RMS-scaled):

position on a Volumetric Scalar field, in this case the Q-Criterion:

16.59.13 X/Y Plots of data other than Topos
The modal dataset contain also Amplitude & Chronos plus various quantities derived from those.
To see these graphically click 'Open as XY-Plot' (
) in the toolbar.
This will open a XY-plot with a number of different curves. Double-Click it to get a list of the data
available and choose which to show on the x-axis, which on the y-axis and which (if any) to hide:
PAGE|739
This lists all the information that a POD mode contains beyond the Topos. The example above has
been set up to plot Chronos as a function of Time and should give a plot like this:
PAGE|740
The first figure shows the result when using the (default) 'Root Sum Square' normalization, while
the second is the same data, but using 'Root Mean Square' normalization instead. As for the Topos
the two are identical except for scale (note the values on the y-axes). For clarity the plots above are
zoomed in on the first ~50 Chronos values instead of showing the full set.
A frequency domain spectrum of the Chronos is also available:
Black curve is the Chronos, red curve the corresponding spectrum. X-axis show frequencies, which
make sense for the spectrum only. The spectrum fulfills Parseval's theorem, so the sum of the
squares of the spectrum correspond to the sum of the squares of the Chronos values. Apart from
that the actual spectrum values have neither units nor physical meaning, the spectrum aims only to
describe frequency content of the Chronos and identify dominant frequencies if any. The example
above is from a mPOD analysis and this spectrum shows a dominant peak just below 200Hz and
no signal content at all outside the range 125-250Hz.
The XY Display Setup can in principle plot any two parameters as functions of one another, it is the
users own responsibility if the resulting curves make any sense. Chronos is for example the only
parameter that makes sense as a function of time and Spectrum the only that makes sense as a
function of frequency. All other parameters can be shown as a function of the Mode Number. Mode
Amplitude distribution is one such plot:
PAGE|741
As before the first figure shows POD results when the default 'Root Sum Square' normalization is
used, while the second figure shows the same data when modes are normalized using 'Root Mean
Square'. As explained above switching from RSS- to RMS-Normalization will increase Topos- and
Chronos-values and decrease modal Amplitudes. This is clearly seen when comparing values on
PAGE|742
the y-axes of the two plots above. For clarity both plots are zoomed in on the first 40 modes instead
of showing the full set.
From the modal amplitude distribution various other mode descriptors can be derived:
l Decay Rate quantify how fast modal amplitudes decay. Noise mode amplitudes tend to level off
so decay rates become small.
l The Energy Fraction is proportional to the square of the Amplitude, but scaled so Sum(Energy
Fractions)=1.0.
l The Accumulated Energy is the sum of Energy Fractions up to and including the current mode.
l The Residual Energy equals 1.0 - Accumulated Energy and describes the amount of energy
NOT covered by modes 1..N.
Please note also that the sum of the squares of the modal amplitudes represents the total energy in
the input data and will be the same regardless of the type of modal analysis performed, POD,
mPOD, sPOD or FMD.
Each of the Chronos furthermore has a temporal Lag-1 Auto-Correlation, quantifying the degree of
similarity between temporal neighbors:
This somewhat unusual formula is in fact the intra-class correlation exploiting the knowledge that
both values in each pair come from one and the same distribution.
The Lag-1 Auto-Correlation of a Chronos quantifies the degree of similarity (coherence) between
consecutive Cronos-values. A pure DC-mode, where all Chronos-values are equal, has a Lag-1
AutoCorrelation of 1.0, while temporally well-resolved modes will produces AC1-values that are
"large", but smaller than one. If the Chronos evaluated is a pure, noise-free sine or cosine, the AC1-
value equals the cosine of the phase angle between consecutive samples. If for example there is 8
samples/cycle the phase angle between consecutive samples will be 360°/8=45° and the Lag1-
AutoCorrelation will become AC1=cos(45°)=0.71. At the Nyquist limit of 2 samples/cycle there will
be 360°/2=180° between consecutive samples and the Lag-1 Auto-Correlation will become AC1-
1=cos(180°)=-1. This is very unlikely in practice, but negative AC1-values are not unusual among
PAGE|743
the higher order modes. In a temporally well resolved experiment the first few modes should how-
ever produce AC1-values in the range 0.5-1.0.
High and low Lag-1 Auto-Correlations can for example originate from a set of Topos and Chronos
such as these:
PAGE|744
Top: Topos. Bottom: Chronos. Left: High AC1-values. Right: Low AC1-values.
The next parameter derived directly from the Chronos is the Kurtosis of each mode. The Kurtosis is
the 4th order standardized moment of the Chronos:
The Kurtosis can tell us if the Chronos-values have reasonably constant magnitude over time or if
they are mostly very small with a few large exceptions.
If Chronos is constant over time (as in the DC-Mode for example), Kurtosis will become 1.0.
If Chronos describe a clean, noise-free sine-wave, Kurtosis will become 1.5.
If Chronos describe white noise with Gaussian distribution, Kurtosis will be around 3.0.
Some textbooks subtract 3 from the formula above to get 'Excess Kurtosis'; -This will be zero for a
normal distribution, negative for distributions that are more 'Peaked' than the normal distribution
and positive for distributions that are more 'Flat'.
PAGE|745
Modes with Kurtosis-values significantly above 3 may be caused by and describe only (undetected)
outliers in the input data. Such modes should of course be excluded from further analysis and the
Kurtosis is intended as a tool to aid the user in identifying them. Please note however that being a
fourth order moment, Kurtosis is a statistical quantity that may depend heavily on the number of
samples (i.e. the number of input datasets). In the figure above it appears for example that Kur-
tosis-values in the range 2-4 are perfectly normal, so one may question whether the single Kur-
tosis-value exceeding 5 is 'Significantly' bigger than 3. If in doubt please take a look at the
corresponding Chronos and/or Topos to see whether it contains evenly distributed noise or a (few)
dominant outlier(s).
The last four mode descriptors are derived from the Chronos spectra, quantifying the 1-50-99 per-
centiles of spectral power and the 98% occupied bandwidth. Mirroring the spectrum and squaring
the amplitudes gives a single sided power spectrum and the 1-percentile is simply the frequency
below which 1% of the total power is located (or, more interestingly, the frequency above which
99% of the power is located). The 50-percentile is the spectral median: Half the signal energy is
below this frequency and half above it. Obviously the 99-percentile is the frequency below which
99% of the spectral energy is contained and the 98% occupied bandwidth is simply the distance
between the 1 & 99 percentiles. The spectral energy percentiles interpolate linearly over each fre-
quency bin so for FMD results, where all signal is in one distinct bin, the 1-50-99 percentiles approx-
imate left, center and right side of the frequency bin in question. The finite and nonzero bin width
means that for the frequency bin centered at f=0 the 1-percentile is negative and likewise a fre-
quency bin centered at the Nyquist limit will have a 99-percentile exceeding that limit. There will
always be a frequency bin centered at f=0, while a frequency bin centered at the Nyquist limit will
exist only if analysis is applied on an even number of snapshots. For an odd number of snapshots
the Nyquist limit will be at the edge of the last frequency bin.
PAGE|746
The plots above illustrate the 1-50-99 percentiles of the various Chronos Spectra from Classic
POD, Multi-Scale POD, Spectral POD and Fourier Mode Decomposition of the same data.
For each mode a vertical line from top to bottom goes through the 99 percentile (red), the 50 per-
centile (grey) and 1 percentile (blue). It also passes one of the green markers indicating the energy
fraction of the mode in question. Frequency percentiles refer to the left (linear) y axis, energy frac-
tions to the right (logarithmic) one.
For the POD results the red 99 percentile markers tend to be at the top and the blue 1 percentile
markers at the bottom, confirming that POD Chronos are broadband with respect to frequency con-
tent. For the mPOD results are clearly clustered in 5 (user defined) frequency bands. For the
sPOD red and blue markers are pulled towards the grey diagonal line and for FMD they are just
above and below the diagonal.
Using the spectral median frequencies as primary axis can give a sort of spectrum showing modal
amplitude as a function of median frequency (vertical axis is logarithmic):
PAGE|747
Apart from the very dominant DC there is clearly a peak between 70 & 75Hz. Please note that for
FMD data there will be two modes at each distinct frequency except the DC and the Nyquist limit
where there is at most one. This is because the approach with frequency domain masking gen-
erates two real modes as opposed to traditional Fourier analysis, which would have created one
complex mode for each frequency bin. The two real modes corresponds to real and imaginary parts
of the complex mode and represent 90° phase shifted versions of the same spatio-temporal struc-
ture.
Please note that even for FMD analysis the output modes remain sorted by descending amplitude.
That's why lines have been switched off in the plot above as they would just be cris-crossing back
and forth in a confusing manner.
16.59.14 Numerical display
Apart from graphical displays, the POD modes can of course be displayed numerically as well:
The first 16 columns are unique to the Modal dataset, describing Chronos as a function of Time,
Spectrum as a function of Frequency and Amplitudes, Decay Rates, Energies, Lag-1 Auto-Cor-
relations and Kurtosis plus power spectral percentiles and occupied bandwidth as a function of
Mode Number. The remaining columns describe the Topos in the same way normal image, scalar
or vector maps are displayed numerically.
Browsing through the modes one by one will show that apart from the Topos, Chronos and Spec-
trum-values are the only ones changing. The remaining parameters are an overview and shared by
all modes.
16.59.15 Input requirements
Modal Analysis can in principle be applied to any image, scalar or vectormap ensemble, but mean-
ingful Topos require input that is spatially well resolved. Similarly meaningful Chronos require input
PAGE|748
that is temporally well resolved. If the flow being measured contain frequencies above half the
sampling rate of the measuring system, aliasing is to be expected and will affect modal results also.
16.59.16 Numeric Export of Modal results
To visualize modal results outside of DynamicStudio or process them further they can be exported
to text files using 'Numeric Export'.
This is done by first selecting the ensemble with the POD results in the database and then picking
'Export' in the File or Context menu:
Either way opens the Numeric Export dialog:
PAGE|749
PAGE|750
At the top of the dialog specify where to put the exported files, what to call them and what index
number should be assigned to the first one (it will increase by one for each file).
Also specify file type from a number of predefined options, in the example above Tecplot has been
chosen. (Read more about it here: "Numeric Export" on page 1009).
The Numeric Export more or less makes a copy of the Numerical Display, which for Modal Analysis
results is a bit unusual:
As explained above the first 2 columns describe timestamps and Chronos for each mode, while the
next 2 are frequencies and spectrum derived from the first two and the next 11 columns are an over-
view common for all modes and remain the same when browsing through them. The remaining
columns describe the spatial distribution of data in each Topos the same way parent data would be
shown numerically. The exact content thus depend on whether parent data was images, scalar
maps or vector maps as in the example used here.
In the first two columns there is one row per timestep and in the next two one line per frequency bin.
The number of lines in these two are always the same, but there's no 1:1 correspondence between
any specific timestep and frequency bin even if they happen to share the same line. The next 12
columns provide an overview of key figures for each mode, primarily the modal amplitude and prop-
erties derived from that, followed by properties derived from the Chronos or the corresponding
Spectrum. For these each line contain properties for one specific mode.
But there is no direct connection between the timestep/frequency, the modal info and the data
points that share a common row.
Think of the Numerical Display as separate tables shown side by side. Normally the number of
modes match the number of timesteps, while the number of datapoints will typically be higher, so
the first 16 columns will typically have some empty space below the last timestep/mode. In some
cases there may be fewer modes than timesteps in which case this becomes very clear:
Thinking of the Modal results as separate tables it makes sense to also export them separately.
With the check-boxes in the bottom left side of the export recipe choose what data to include or
exclude in the exported files. To export mode timesteps and corresponding Chronos values
uncheck all but the first two:
PAGE|751
Frequency and Spectrum can of course be included also or a separate export made for those two.
To export the modal energy distribution and the like select only the next 12 modes:
Of course Kurtosis, Occupied Bandwidth or other parameters can be excluded if not needed.
To finally export the spatial distribution in the Topos check the desired quantities among the remain-
ing columns:
This example is set up to export only the metric locations and the vector components, while grid
index positions as well as pixel positions are excluded.
This example was based on Modal Analysis of a series of vector maps. With POD analysis of e.g.
Image or Scalar maps instead the Topos will of course change accordingly.
For all of these exports the format such as number of decimals for each of the parameters exported
can of course . Read more about formatting in "Numeric Export" on page 1009.
PAGE|752
Exporting Image Topos
Numeric Export organize the data in the same way as the Numeric Display. This spreadsheet style
layout is well suited for Modal Amplitudes, Chronos and things derived from those. It is also well
suited for Topos when analyzing Scalar or Vector maps, but not when the parent data are images.
In that case please consider using "Image Export" on page 1006 for the Topos instead. Image
Topos will be floating point, so make sure to choose TIFF as it is the only image format supporting
floating point pixel values. Please note that most other software does not support or recognize float-
ing point images, so the exported TIFF images will most likely appear very dark or truncated black-
/white when shown or opened from e.g. Windows File Explorer. This is purely a display problem,
the files are in fact OK and will display correctly if imported into e.g. MATLAB or other software that
support and recognize floating point TIFF images.
16.59.17 Troubleshooting, Tips & Tricks
Modal Analysis will try to load as much of the input data as possible into RAM. But for long time-
series of large datasets they may not fit and DynamicStudio will rely on disk-swapping to access all
of the necessary data. Analysis time will go up, but should complete eventually. This requires how-
ever that 'Virtual Memory' is enabled and allocated in Windows Settings.
